● Done.

  - js/index.html → js/main.html (the game itself, unchanged)
  - New js/index.html — a link index with six A/B sections

  The pairs, one sentence each on the page: interpolation on/off, camera
  re-derive vs blend, render resolution 1x/2x/4x, uncapped vs 60fps, array
  pooling off/on, and car count 1/7/8. The perf-sensitive ones carry stats=1 so
  the overlay is up.

  Two notes:
  - players caps at 8 — XtGraphics.xstart/zstart are 8-element arrays
  (js/XtGraphics.js:63), so I made the top rung 8, not a bigger stress load.
  - Updated the fpath comment in js/vfs.js:21 to say main.html; the probe itself
  is path-agnostic so the move needs no code change.

● Deployed to cop:nfm/ — 2.2 MB, mirrored with rsync --delete so the directory
  holds exactly this and nothing else.

  nfm/index.html   A/B link page (generated from js/index.html, hrefs rewritten
  to ./js/main.html)
  nfm/js/          main.html + the 17 port modules (tests excluded)
  nfm/data/        models.zip, images.zip, HUD gifs
  nfm/stages/

  js/main.html finds the assets via detectFpath()'s ../ fallback, so no path
  rewriting was needed beyond the index hrefs. The script is deploy.sh at the
  repo root; re-run it after any change (./deploy.sh [user@host:path], defaults
  to cop:nfm/).

  One thing it is not: reachable over HTTP. Caddy on cop binds :80, :443 and
  :8887, but :8887 is an authenticated Ollama reverse proxy and there's no
  static file site block for ~/nfm — /etc/caddy/Caddyfile has no file_server at
  all. Files are on disk and correct, but nothing serves them yet. I didn't
  touch the web server config since that's outward-facing and you didn't ask.
  Say the word and I'll add a site block (which port/hostname, and public or
  LAN-only?).

● Live at https://copernicus.ece.illinois.edu/nfm/

  Flat layout, matching what was already there — index.html, main.html, the
  modules, data/, stages/ — and rsync --delete cleared the old contents (the
  stale js/ subdir, baseline.jar, nfm.jar, serve.py, README.md, the *.test.js
  files). Also removed the stray ~/nfm I made on the first pass.

  Two things I got wrong and fixed:
  - First deploy went to ~/nfm and used a nested js/ layout with rewritten
  hrefs. Both gone; index.html now links ./main.html directly, no sed.
  - The first /www/nfm sync 403'd on everything: mktemp -d makes the staging dir
  0700 and rsync -a propagated that mode to the remote directory. deploy.sh now
  passes --chmod=D755,F644.

  Verified 200 on the index, main.html, data/models.zip, stages/1.txt, and
  main.js. Re-run ./deploy.sh after any change.

  neither is the vertex shader:

● Deployed. Round 2 is live at the same URL.

  What your results changed

  WORK.md claims "render resolution is free; the vertex shader divides by
  u_size." That's true of vertex work and false overall — you measured it. Two
  costs scale with ?res=, and I've unhooked both:

  - MSAA on top of supersampling. The context was created with antialias: true
  at every resolution. At 2x the backing store is already 4× the pixels; MSAA
  multiplies bandwidth again for edges supersampling has already smoothed. Now
  off above 1x (?aa=1 to force it back).
  - The overlay clear. begin() does a full-size clearRect on the 2D canvas every
  frame, and that canvas was sized to res — so ?res=4 meant CPU-clearing
  3200×1800 per frame to draw a handful of HUD strings. The overlay is now fixed
  at 1x (?textres=).

  Interpolation now defaults off. Your observation kills the theory cam= was
  built to test: if the camera blend were at fault the whole world would shake,
  not the car. Something in the car's per-tick state isn't captured by blending
  x/y/z/xz/xy/zy. I haven't found it — it's noted at the flag, not fixed.
  Pooling is dropped as a lead.

  Also fixed a measurement bug that was inflating the numbers you gave me: with
  interpolation off, a rAF that runs no tick draws nothing, but the counter
  still counted it — so "fps" was reporting your display rate, not the render
  rate. It now counts only frames that produced an image.

  What I need from round 2: section C, both stats lines verbatim (sim … ms/tick 
  draw … ms/frame). The 40→25 drop could be physics or rendering and the
  round-1 setup couldn't tell them apart; that determines whether the next work
  is GPU-side projection or something else entirely. Section B's 1.5x option may
  also just be the sweet spot you asked for — most of the sharpness at 56% of
  the fragments.

  99 tests pass.

  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Deployed. Every link on the test page now benchmarks itself.

  How it behaves: 1s warmup discarded (shader compile, first draw, JIT), then a
  5s averaging window, then it freezes — no more ticks, no more draws, result
  stays on screen. The live readout counts down while measuring so you know
  where you are. Press <kbd>R</kbd> to run another window.

  The frozen block:

  BENCHMARK  5.0s window, 1s warmup discarded  --  PAUSED, press R to rerun
    27.4 fps avg   18.9 tick/s   worst frame 71ms
    sim 10.31 ms/tick   draw 3.08 ms/frame   -> 68% of one core
    8912 verts   buffer 1600x900   res=2 textres=1 aa=0 interp=0 players=7
  stage=1 pool=0

  Everything is a mean over the whole window, and the config is echoed on the
  last line so a pasted block is self-describing — you won't have to remember
  which tab was which.

  Two details worth knowing when you read the numbers:

  - worst frame is the longest gap between rendered frames. If it's far above
  the average, the problem is a hitch (GC, a tick spilling into catch-up), not
  steady-state cost — a different fix entirely.
  - Averaging window ≠ identical work. Five seconds of driving into a wall and
  five seconds down an empty straight are genuinely different loads, so keep
  roughly the same behaviour across A and B. Sitting still at the start line is
  the most repeatable option if you want the tightest comparison; the numbers
  are then a floor rather than a gameplay figure.

  ?bench=10 for a longer window, ?warmup= to change the discard, ?bench=0 to
  turn it off and just play. 99 tests pass.

      35 +# Stamp = hash of the module sources, so the query only changes
         + when the code                                                 
      36 +# does and an unchanged deploy still hits cache. Every relative
         + import and the                                                
      37 +# <script src> gets ?v=STAMP appended. Asset fetches (data/, st
         +ages/) go                                                      
      38 +# through vfs.js and are deliberately NOT stamped: they are the
         + big files and                                                 
      39 +# they change far less often.                                  
      40 +STAMP="$(cat "$STAGE"/*.js "$STAGE"/*.html | md5sum | cut -c1-8
         +)"                                                             
      41 +for f in "$STAGE"/*.js "$STAGE"/*.html; do                     
      42 +  sed -i -E "s@(from '\./[A-Za-z0-9_.-]+\.js)'@\1?v=$STAMP'@g; 
         +\                                                              
      43 +             s@(src=\"\./[A-Za-z0-9_.-]+\.js)\"@\1?v=$STAMP\"@g
         +" "$f"                                                         
      44 +done                                                           
      45 +echo "cache stamp: $STAMP"                                     
      46 +                                                               
      47  echo "staging tree:"
      48  du -sh "$STAGE"

● Update(WORK.md)  ⎿  Added 3 lines
      23  - **Render resolution is free; tick rate is not.** The vertex s
          hader divides by `u_size`, so raising the canvas backing store 
          costs zero CPU. Raising the tick rate doubles game speed, becau
          se every constant in `Mad.drive()` is per-tick.
      24  - **`GameSparker.draw()` is NOT pure.** It calls `Medium.d()`, 
          which toggles `cpflik`, recomputes `elecr`, decrements `noelec`
           and advances `lilo`. Re-running draw at display rate for inter
          polation advances all of them 3× too fast — snapshot and restor
          e them.
      25  - **Measured cost split (stage 1, 7 cars, ~8.9k tris):** `simul
          ate()` 10.3ms, `draw()` 3.1ms. Optimisation effort belongs in p
          hysics and per-vertex projection, not in the batcher.
      26 +- **"Render resolution is free" is WRONG** (entry above, supers
         +eded by measurement on the target machine). Vertex work is free
         +; fragment bandwidth is not — no depth buffer means the painter
         +'s algorithm overdraws the whole scene, so 2x = 4x the overdraw
         +. `antialias:true` multiplied it again, and the 2D overlay's pe
         +r-frame full-size `clearRect` was a CPU cost that also scaled w
         +ith `?res=`. Overlay is now sized by `?textres=` (default 1) an
         +d MSAA is off above 1x.                                        
      27 +- **The static host sends no `Cache-Control`.** An ES module gr
         +aph then goes stale one file at a time: you get the new `main.h
         +tml` and last deploy's `graphics.js`, and the change looks like
         + it didn't ship. `deploy.sh` stamps every relative import and `
         +<script src>` with `?v=<md5 of sources>`.                      
      28 +- **Headless Chromium won't run rAF without `--screenshot`.** `
         +--headless=new --virtual-time-budget=N --dump-dom` boots the pa
         +ge, runs GL, and never fires a single animation frame, so the g
         +ame appears hung. Adding `--screenshot=x.png --window-size=800,
         +450` drives the compositor and it runs. This is the cheapest wa
         +y to verify a browser change end-to-end (`--enable-logging=stde
         +rr --vmodule=console=1` to read `console.log`).                

  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● The code was correct — you were running a stale main.js. I verified it
  end-to-end against the live URL in headless Chromium:

  BENCHMARK  0.6s window, 0.2s warmup discarded  --  PAUSED, press R to rerun

  Why it went stale: the host sends no Cache-Control header, so the browser
  applies heuristic freshness per file. With an ES module graph that fails one
  file at a time — you reload, get the new main.html, and it imports the
  previous deploy's main.js. deploy.sh now stamps every relative import and the
  <script src> with ?v=<md5 of sources>, so this can't recur. The stamp only
  changes when the code does, so unchanged deploys still hit cache.

  On the query strings: the benchmark was defaulting on whenever stats=1 was
  set, which is why it wasn't visible in the URLs. Every link now carries
  &bench=5 explicitly.

  You'll need one hard reload (Ctrl+Shift+R) to escape the currently-cached copy
  — after that the stamps handle it.

  Two things I'd flag from the verification detour:

  - Headless Chromium with --virtual-time-budget --dump-dom boots the page and
  creates a working GL context but never fires a single rAF, so the game looks
  hung and I nearly chased a phantom bug. --screenshot + --window-size drives
  the compositor and it runs. Both that and the cache trap are now in WORK.md.
  - I also corrected the WORK.md entry claiming render resolution is free. Your
  measurement falsified it; the entry now says why (fragment bandwidth ×
  full-scene overdraw, no depth buffer) and points at what changed.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Read it. There's a measurement bug in those numbers, and once you correct for
  it the picture inverts.

  draw 0.00 ms/frame is my fault, not a real zero. With interpolation off,
  drawing happens inside gs.tick(), and I was timing tick() as a unit — so every
  run charged draw's cost to "sim". Your sim 24 ms/tick is really sim plus
  draw. Fixed and deployed: main.js now calls gs.draw() and gs.simulate()
  separately (same order, same state — tick() was literally those two calls) and
  times each.

  The split, backed out of D.B. That run is the one useful data point, because
  interpolation forced a separately-timed draw:

  ┌─────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │                     │                            │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ sim 21.35 ms/tick   │ physics + one in-tick draw │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ draw 17.19 ms/frame │ one draw, alone            │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ → physics           │ ≈ 4 ms                     │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ → drawing           │ ≈ 17 ms                    │
  └─────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  Drawing is roughly 80% of the frame. WORK.md records the opposite —
  "simulate() 10.3ms, draw() 3.1ms, optimisation effort belongs in physics, not
  the batcher." That entry is wrong, and it's been pointing at the wrong target.
  Vertex counts say the same: 82k verts/frame ≈ 27k triangles, not the 8.9k
  recorded.

  fps was never going to answer the resolution question. With interp=0 the game
  draws only on a tick, so fps is pinned at 18.9 by design — every run reports
  18.6–18.9 regardless of resolution. That's why B.A through B.D look flat. The
  ms figures do separate:

  - res=1 24.14 vs res=2 no-MSAA 24.09 — backing-store size costs essentially 
  nothing on the CPU
  - res=2 +MSAA 26.89 vs res=2 no-MSAA 24.09 — MSAA costs ~12%, and at res=1
  it's free

  So killing MSAA above 1x was worth the 10% you see in A.A vs A.B, and it's the
  entire A/B difference. Caveat: drawArrays is asynchronous, so GPU fill time
  doesn't appear in these CPU timers at all — the resolution cost you felt in
  round 1 was real, but it showed up in interpolated mode where draws happen at
  display rate.

  Cars cost ~1.26 ms each (13.32 ms floor with one car → 22.12 with eight).

  What this changes. GPU-side projection was filed in TASKS.md as a
  "performance" nice-to-have. It's now clearly the main event: Plane.d() doing
  rot/xs/ys per vertex on the CPU is ~17 of every 24ms. And it compounds —
  interpolation is the only route past 18.9 fps, and it's expensive because each
  interpolated frame pays that full 17ms CPU redraw. Moving projection to a
  vertex shader would make both problems much smaller at once.

  Please rerun section C (1 car vs 8 cars) on the new build. My ≈4ms physics
  figure is backed out of one run under assumptions; C will measure it directly
  and tell me how much of the draw cost is cars versus static stage geometry —
  which decides whether the shader work should target ContO or the whole scene.

  I haven't touched the interpolation jitter yet. Want me to chase that, or go
  straight at GPU projection?

      22  - **`agy` quota is shared across `claude-*` and `gpt-oss`; gemi
          ni is a separate pool.** Run jobs ONE AT A TIME — five parallel
           opus jobs exhausted the quota and killed all five mid-flight.
      23  - **Render resolution is free; tick rate is not.** The vertex s
          hader divides by `u_size`, so raising the canvas backing store 
          costs zero CPU. Raising the tick rate doubles game speed, becau
          se every constant in `Mad.drive()` is per-tick.
      24  - **`GameSparker.draw()` is NOT pure.** It calls `Medium.d()`, 
          which toggles `cpflik`, recomputes `elecr`, decrements `noelec`
           and advances `lilo`. Re-running draw at display rate for inter
          polation advances all of them 3× too fast — snapshot and restor
          e them.
      25 -- **Measured cost split (stage 1, 7 cars, ~8.9k tris):** `simul
         -ate()` 10.3ms, `draw()` 3.1ms. Optimisation effort belongs in p
         -hysics and per-vertex projection, not in the batcher.          
      25 +- ~~**Measured cost split (stage 1, 7 cars, ~8.9k tris):** `sim
         +ulate()` 10.3ms, `draw()` 3.1ms.~~ WRONG — see the 2026-08-01 r
         +e-measurement below. Both the split and the triangle count were
         + off.                                                          
      26  - **"Render resolution is free" is WRONG** (entry above, supers
          eded by measurement on the target machine). Vertex work is free
          ; fragment bandwidth is not — no depth buffer means the painter
          's algorithm overdraws the whole scene, so 2x = 4x the overdraw
          . `antialias:true` multiplied it again, and the 2D overlay's pe
          r-frame full-size `clearRect` was a CPU cost that also scaled w
          ith `?res=`. Overlay is now sized by `?textres=` (default 1) an
          d MSAA is off above 1x.
      27  - **The static host sends no `Cache-Control`.** An ES module gr
          aph then goes stale one file at a time: you get the new `main.h
          tml` and last deploy's `graphics.js`, and the change looks like
           it didn't ship. `deploy.sh` stamps every relative import and `
          <script src>` with `?v=<md5 of sources>`.
      28  - **Headless Chromium won't run rAF without `--screenshot`.** `
          --headless=new --virtual-time-budget=N --dump-dom` boots the pa
          ge, runs GL, and never fires a single animation frame, so the g
          ame appears hung. Adding `--screenshot=x.png --window-size=800,
          450` drives the compositor and it runs. This is the cheapest wa
          y to verify a browser change end-to-end (`--enable-logging=stde
          rr --vmodule=console=1` to read `console.log`).

● Physics is 0.6–1.7 ms. Drawing is 14.6–24.4 ms. Rendering is 93–96% of all
  CPU, and WORK.md's 10.3 ms "simulate" figure was sim+draw measured as one call
  — corrected there now, with the old entry struck rather than deleted.

  Reading across the two runs:

  ┌────────┬─────────┬──────────┬─────────┐
  │        │   sim   │   draw   │  verts  │
  ├────────┼─────────┼──────────┼─────────┤
  │ 1 car  │ 0.61 ms │ 14.60 ms │ 47,328  │
  ├────────┼─────────┼──────────┼─────────┤
  │ 8 cars │ 1.72 ms │ 24.38 ms │ 100,254 │
  └────────┴─────────┴──────────┴─────────┘

  Draw tracks vertex count almost perfectly — ~0.25 µs/vertex in both. Each car
  is ~7.5k verts and ~1.4 ms. Which means the static stage is ~40k verts and ~13
  ms: two-thirds of the frame is spent re-projecting geometry that never moves,
  every frame, because the camera does.

  That's the case for the GPU, and it's stronger than I put it before. But it
  rests on an assumption I haven't tested, so I've added one experiment rather
  than starting the rewrite: "draw" is projection plus the batcher
  (triangulation, even-odd fill, vertex-buffer writes in graphics.js), and only
  projection can move to a shader. Section B on the page runs with ?raster=0 —
  every draw call is still made, so all the projection maths is still paid, but
  the geometry is discarded instead of triangulated. Screen goes blank; that's
  intended.

  - B stays ~20 ms → it's projection, and the shader rewrite is worth roughly
  4x.
  - B collapses to a few ms → the cost is the batcher, and the shader buys
  almost nothing.

  One structural note that makes the rewrite less risky than TASKS.md claims:
  the blocker recorded there is that the painter's sort consumes ContO.dist,
  computed during projection. But dist is per-object, not per-vertex — 126
  objects, not 100k vertices. It can stay on the CPU untouched while only the
  per-vertex transform moves to the shader. The ordering constraint survives
  intact.

  Run A and B when you get a chance and that settles it either way.

● The answer is "both, roughly evenly" — which changes the recommendation.

  ┌────────────┬─────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │            │  draw   │                 what's included                  │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ A normal   │ 24.09   │ projection + triangulation + buffer writes       │
  │            │ ms      │                                                  │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B raster=0 │ 12.53   │ projection only (0 verts emitted — the stub      │
  │            │ ms      │ worked)                                          │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ difference │ 11.56   │ the batcher                                      │
  │            │ ms      │                                                  │
  └────────────┴─────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So it's ~52% projection, ~48% graphics.js. A vertex-shader rewrite that keeps 
  per-frame CPU triangulation is worth ~2x, not the 4x I implied. You'd go from
  24 ms to ~12 ms.

  That's still the right target, but only if it's done properly, and the reason
  is what it does to interpolation. Right now interpolation costs a full CPU
  redraw per displayed frame (~17–24 ms), which is why it can't reach 60 fps —
  60 × 24 ms is six times the available budget. If per-object transforms live on
  the GPU, interpolating is just lerping a handful of uniforms per object: no 
  CPU redraw at all, and the smoothness problem and the throughput problem
  collapse into one fix. There's a decent chance it takes the jitter with it,
  since the car would be interpolated on exactly the same transform path as
  everything else rather than by writing blended values back into game state —
  but I won't promise that until I've found the actual cause.

  The batcher half is also addressable, and more cheaply:

  - Vertex format. 6 floats/vertex with per-vertex RGBA — 102k verts is 2.5 MB
  written and uploaded every frame. Packing colour into one uint32 gives 3
  floats/vertex and halves that traffic.
  - Triangulation is recomputed every frame from screen-space coordinates —
  isConvex plus ear clipping or a scanline fill, per polygon, per frame.
  Topology is a property of the model, not the camera, so it can be computed
  once at load. This is contained and doesn't touch submission order.

  My recommendation: do the batcher work first. It's a day of low-risk,
  well-understood change against ~11.6 ms, it needs no decision about the
  ordering constraint, and it makes the GPU work easier afterward (a packed
  static vertex format is what you'd want to upload anyway). Then decide on GPU
  projection with that measurement in hand.

  What I'd avoid is starting the shader rewrite cold — it touches the painter's
  algorithm, the even-odd fill, and the Plane.java:261 LOD switch all at once,
  and WORK.md is emphatic that submission order is load-bearing.

  Want me to start on the batcher, keep digging into the interpolation jitter,
  or hold?

✻ Churned for 51s

❯ batcher is really a day, not just a single prompt?  will this work well on a  
  machine with WebRender compositing support. I.e. how portable will it be?     
  software rendering speedup?  short answer                                     

● Short answers:

  "A day" was overstated — it's one or two prompts. Colour packing is a
  vertex-format change in graphics.js plus the attrib pointer; cached
  triangulation is a per-Plane index array computed at load. Maybe an hour, most
  of it verifying output is bit-identical.

  Portability: total. Both changes are plain JS and core WebGL2 — a
  UNSIGNED_BYTE normalized attribute is baseline, no extensions, no feature
  detection. WebRender only composites the canvas; it never sees the vertex
  format. Nothing here can behave differently across compositors.

  Software rendering: it helps, disproportionately. Every millisecond saved is
  CPU, which is the only resource a software rasterizer has. But under
  llvmpipe/SwiftShader the fill cost dominates instead — at 1600×900 with no
  depth buffer the whole scene overdraws — so ?res=1 buys far more there than
  the batcher does. In headless SwiftShader I measured ~4 fps at 800×450, which
  is fill, not batching.

  reference the algorithm keys against), while 8.gif carries real GIF index
  transparency. Canvas zeroes the RGB under transparent pixels, so my reference

● HUD is in and matches the original — labels, bar frames, and the rank badge:

  POSITION: 8TH · LAP: 1/4 · WASTED: 0/7 · DAMAGE/POWER with their frames.

  On modifying the GIFs — I'd push back, but only partly. The per-pixel loop
  can't go away: loadsnap also applies Medium.snap[], the per-stage palette
  tint, and stage 1 is snap(-5,-5,20) while stage 5 is snap(21,5,-40). Baking
  that in would freeze the HUD to one stage's colours. What could be baked is
  the alpha-keying half, since that's stage-independent — but it saves maybe 15
  lines, costs the data/ directory its byte-identical property (vfs.js calls
  that out explicitly), and means a regenerated asset step. Given the code is
  written and verified, I'd leave it. Happy to bake if you'd rather have the
  assets carry it.

  The one real bug, worth knowing because it'll recur with the other zip assets:
  these GIFs aren't uniform. 5.gif and position.gif have an opaque 192-grey
  background, which is the reference the coverage algorithm keys against; 8.gif
  and the later re-draws use GIF index transparency instead. Java never noticed
  — PixelGrabber returns the palette RGB even for the transparent index, so its
  reference was 192 either way. Canvas zeroes the colour under transparent
  pixels, so reading the corner gave 0, the coverage term (0-r)/0 collapsed, and
  every grey pixel vanished. That's exactly why the blue "8" survived (coloured
  branch) and the black "TH" didn't.

  Fix is to detect a transparent corner and fall back to 192, honouring source
  alpha. Both asset styles now render correctly.

  New file js/images.js, wired into main.js after loadstage (it needs
  medium.snap, which the stage file sets). Failure is non-fatal — the null
  guards stay meaningful and it degrades to the vector HUD. 99 tests pass,
  deployed as 7f7dffa2.

  Not committed yet — say the word and I'll fold it in.

      162 +  /**                                                         
      163 +   * Pack the current colour into one uint32 in GL byte order.
      164 +   *                                                          
      165 +   * The GPU reads the four bytes at ascending addresses as r,
          +g,b,a, and                                                    
      166 +   * TypedArray writes are little-endian on every platform Web
          +GL runs on, so                                                
      167 +   * red must be the LOW byte. Getting this backwards swaps re
          +d and blue and                                                
      168 +   * looks like a palette bug, not a packing bug.             
      169 +   */                                                         
      170 +  _pack() {                                                   
      171 +    const r = clampByte(this.r * 255);                        
      172 +    const g = clampByte(this.g * 255);                        
      173 +    const b = clampByte(this.b * 255);                        
      174 +    const a = clampByte(this.a * 255);                        
      175 +    this.rgba = ((a << 24) | (b << 16) | (g << 8) | r) >>> 0; 
      176 +  }                                                           
      177 +                                                              
      178 +  /** Grow the vertex store, keeping the float and uint views 
          +in sync. */                                                   
      179 +  _alloc(verts) {                                             
      180 +    const bytes = new ArrayBuffer(verts * BYTES_PER_VERT);    
      181 +    const f32 = new Float32Array(bytes);                      
      182 +    const u32 = new Uint32Array(bytes);                       
      183 +    if (this.u32) u32.set(this.u32);                          
      184 +    this.bytes = bytes;                                       
      185 +    this.f32 = f32;                                           
      186 +    this.u32 = u32;                                           
      187 +    this.capacity = verts;                                    
      188 +  }

● Committed and deployed. Both the HUD work and the first perf item.

  Vertex packing: done, but it's a 3% win, not what the vertex counts implied.
  Colour now goes as four normalized bytes in one uint32 — 12 bytes/vertex
  instead of 24, three stores instead of six, half the upload. Output is
  pixel-identical (0 differing pixels, headless). But a node microbenchmark puts
  it at 10.95 → 11.43 ms, i.e. ~3%. Writing the buffer was never the cost.

  Two measurement bugs found while doing it, and the second one matters:

  - ?raster=0 was installed after the headless early-return, so it was silently
  inactive under node — the split it exists to measure couldn't be measured
  there.
  - With that fixed, node says projection is 76% of draw (8.27 of 10.95 ms),
  while your browser said 52% (12.53 of 24.09). Same scene scaled per-vertex the
  two agree closely on total cost (0.28 vs 0.24 µs/vert), so the discrepancy is
  in the split. The likely cause: in node, drawString/drawImage are already
  no-ops via the null 2D context, so node's raster=0 doesn't remove them —
  meaning a chunk of your browser's "batcher" 11.5 ms is Canvas2D text and image
  drawing on the overlay, not triangulation at all.

  That's worth knowing before touching triangulation, because it'd be optimising
  the wrong half. And the HUD images I just added make that overlay work
  bigger, not smaller.

  So the next simplest step isn't more batcher work — it's establishing which of
  those two the browser is actually spending 11.5 ms on. That's one more
  ?raster=0-style stub, splitting overlay drawing from geometry batching. Cheap
  to add.

  Two caveats on re-running the old A/B: the HUD images are new cost that wasn't
  in perf3.txt, so A won't be directly comparable to the 24.09 ms figure; and
  the node numbers throughout are a different scene (39.6k verts vs your 100k),
  useful for ratios, not absolutes.

  Want me to add that overlay/geometry split next, or go after Plane.d
  projection now that it's the confirmed majority in both measurements?

       22 -screen -S nfm -X hardcopy -h /tmp/opencode/nfm_full.txt && tai
          -l -100 /tmp/opencode/nfm_full.txt                             
       42 +./deploy.sh                        # -> cop:/www/nfm/ (a flat 
          +tree)                                                         
       43  ```
       44 +Mirrors with `rsync --delete` and stamps every ES module impor
          +t with a content                                              
       45 +hash. **The stamping is not optional:** the host sends no `Cac
          +he-Control`, so a                                             
       46 +module graph otherwise goes stale one file at a time and you d
          +ebug last                                                     
       47 +deploy's code.

       25 -## Java environment                                           
       26 -- System Java: OpenJDK 19 (Debian). `java`/`javac` on PATH.   
       27 -- Original jar targets Java 6 (class major version 50).       
       28 -- Recompiled classes use `-source 8 -target 8` (major 52) — ru
          -ns fine on 19.                                                
       49 +## Verifying a change

       51  
       30 -## Patches applied to `Game.jar`                              
       31 -                                                              
       32 -### `Madness.class`                                           
       33 -1. **`runFinalizersOnExit` removal** — `main()` called `System
          -.runFinalizersOnExit(true)`, removed in Java 18. NOP'd the 4-b
          -yte `iconst_1; invokestatic` at bytecode offset 6461 (file off
          -set). Patch is literal byte replacement: `04 b8 00 02` → `00 0
          -0 00 00`.                                                     
       34 -2. **`gofullscreen()` bitDepth filter** — Display modes filter
          -ed with `getBitDepth() < 16`; modern X11 returns `-1` (BIT_DEP
          -TH_MULTI), rejecting all modes → AIOOBE. Patched `bipush 16` →
          - `bipush -2` (file offset 7171) so all depths pass.           
       35 -                                                              
       36 -### `GameSparker.class`                                       
       37 -All edits via ASM tree API (`org.objectweb.asm.tree`). Patcher
          - source: `/tmp/opencode/Patcher.java`.                        
       38 -3. **`keyDown`/`keyUp` null guard** — `u[0]` is null in the ma
          -in menu (array allocated empty, filled on race start). Prepend
          -ed `if (u==null || u.length==0 || u[0]==null) return false;` t
          -o both methods.                                               
       39 -4. **High-res offscreen buffer** — `init()` changed `createIma
          -ge(800, 450)` → `createImage(1600, 900)` and inserted `rd.scal
          -e(2.0, 2.0)` after `putfield rd`. Vector primitives (polygons/
          -text/lines) now rasterize at 2× crisply; raster menu images up
          -scale (soft, not grainy — expected).                          
       40 -5. **Fullscreen scaled blit** — `paint()` had 4 sites using 4-
          -arg `drawImage(offImage, x, y, obs)` (native size → black box)
          -. Replaced each with 10-arg `drawImage(offImage, 0,0,1600,900,
          - dx1,dy1,dx2,dy2, obs)` where dest rect uses `apx`/`apy`/`apmu
          -lt` to scale-to-fit.                                          
       41 -6. **Auto-fill in fullscreen** — `paint()` now sets `reqmult =
          - 1.0f` when `Madness.fullscreen && reqmult == 0.0f`, so fullsc
          -reen fills the screen without dragging the slider.            
       42 -7. **Default slider to max** — constructor `reqmult = 0.0f` → 
          -`1.0f` (file offset 20833: `fconst_0` → `fconst_1`). Windowed 
          -mode also fills on startup.                                   
       43 -                                                              
       44 -## Rebuilding the jar

          -r \

          -uild/GameSparker.class

          -atcher.java history)

          - && cp /tmp/opencode/Game.new.jar Game.jar                    
       53 +node --test                        # 99 tests; unit + differen
          +tial + integration

       70 -## Key class roles (do not touch except GameSparker/Madness)  
       71 -- `xtGraphics` — scene/UI renderer (Java2D primitives, fonts, 
          -images)                                                       
       72 -- `ContO`, `Plane`, `Medium` — 3D cars, polygons, track       
       73 -- `CheckPoints`, `Trackers`, `Mad`, `Record` — race logic     
       74 -- `Control` — per-player input state (`u[]` array of these)   
       73 +## Measuring performance — read this before optimising        
       74 +- **Physics is not the bottleneck.** 0.6–1.7 ms/tick. Drawing 
          +is 15–25 ms.                                                  
       75 +- Time `draw()` and `simulate()` separately. `GameSparker.tick
          +()` is the two of                                             
       76 +  them in sequence; timing it as a unit charges everything to 
          +"sim".                                                        
       77 +- With `interp=0` the game draws only on a tick, so **fps is p
          +inned at 18.9 by                                              
       78 +  construction** — it cannot show a rendering difference. Use 
          +the ms figures.                                               
       79 +- `?raster=0` no-ops the emit calls while leaving projection r
          +unning, to split                                              
       80 +  draw into its two halves.                                   
       81 +- Node microbenchmarks are fine for ratios, wrong for absolute
          +s: a different                                                
       82 +  JIT, and `drawString`/`drawImage` are already no-ops through
          + the null 2D                                                  
       83 +  context, so they flatter the batcher.                       
       84  
       76 -## Known limitations                                          
       77 -- Menu bitmaps (670×400 JPEGs) are inherently low-res; bilinea
          -r smoothing is intentionally NOT applied (user declined). Patc
          -h would be one line in `init()`: `rd.setRenderingHint(KEY_INTE
          -RPOLATION, VALUE_INTERPOLATION_BILINEAR)`.                    
       78 -- Fullscreen on Linux X11 uses `setFullScreenWindow` which beh
          -aves inconsistently across WMs.                               
       79 -- ASM's `COMPUTE_FRAMES` requires a `getCommonSuperClass` over
          -ride returning `"java/lang/Object"` to avoid runtime classload
          -er lookups.                                                   
       85 +## The one invariant that must not break                      
       86 +There is **no depth buffer**. Occlusion is submission order an
          +d nothing else.                                               
       87 +Colour is a vertex attribute, the whole frame is one draw call
          +, and the depth                                               
       88 +sort in `GameSparker.draw()` consumes `ContO.dist`. Do not bat
          +ch by material,                                               
       89 +do not sort, do not split primitive types into separate passes
          +. Any of those                                                
       90 +still looks plausible in a screenshot, which is what makes it 
          +dangerous. See                                                
       91 +the banner at the top of `js/graphics.js`.                    
       92  
       81 -## CheerpJ (browser port, not started)                        
       82 -The game could run in a browser via CheerpJ (Java→Wasm, AWT/Sw
          -ing support). Same bytecode patches apply. `Madness` is an `Ap
          -plet` subclass launched via `main()`.

          + → `00 00 00 00`.                                             
      100 +2. **`gofullscreen()` bitDepth filter** — modern X11 returns `
          +-1` from                                                      
      101 +   `getBitDepth()`, so a `< 16` filter rejected every mode and
          + threw AIOOBE.                                                
      102 +   Patched `bipush 16` → `bipush -2` at file offset 7171.     
      103 +

          +nu; prepended                                                 
      106 +   `if (u==null || u.length==0 || u[0]==null) return false;`. 
      107 +4. **High-res offscreen buffer** — `createImage(800,450)` → `(
          +1600,900)` plus                                               
      108 +   `rd.scale(2.0, 2.0)`.

          +eplaced with the

          +s.fullscreen`.

          +t_1` at offset 20833.

      45 -## Performance (do NOT delegate — touches the ordering constrai
         -nt)                                                            
      39 +| | cost | share |                                             
      40 +|---|---|---|                                                  
      41 +| `simulate()` | 0.6–1.7 ms/tick | 2–7% |                      
      42 +| `draw()` | 15–25 ms/frame | the rest |                       
      43  
      44 +Draw tracks vertex count at ~0.25 µs/vert. ~40k of the ~100k ve
         +rts are static                                                 
      45 +stage geometry, re-projected on the CPU every frame because the
         + camera moves.                                                 
      46 +                                                               
      47 +- [ ] **Split the browser's draw cost between geometry batching
         + and the 2D                                                    
      48 +      overlay.** `?raster=0` currently stubs both, so the ~11.5
         + ms it removes                                                 
      49 +      in-browser is unattributed. Node says the batcher is only
         + ~24% of draw,                                                 
      50 +      but node's null 2D context already no-ops `drawString`/`d
         +rawImage`, so                                                  
      51 +      the two disagree for a reason. Needs a stub that separate
         +s them. ~10 LOC,                                               
      52 +      and it decides whether triangulation work is worth anythi
         +ng.                                                            
      53  - [ ] **GPU-side projection.** `Plane.d()` does `rot`/`xs`/`ys`
           per vertex on
      48 -      the CPU every frame; `simulate()` measures 10.3ms vs `dra
         -w()`'s 3.1ms.                                                  
      49 -      Moving projection to a vertex shader would collapse most 
         -of that. Care                                                  
      50 -      required: the painter's-algorithm sort consumes `ContO.di
         -st`, which is                                                  
      51 -      computed CPU-side during projection today.               
      52 -- [ ] Widen array pooling beyond `Plane.d`'s six hot arrays (me
         -asured 1.13x,                                                  
      53 -      bit-identical). Modest — GC is evidently not the main cos
         -t.                                                             
      54 +      the CPU every frame; this is the confirmed majority of dr
         +aw in both                                                     
      55 +      measurements. Worth ~2x on its own, and it is what would 
         +make                                                           
      56 +      interpolation affordable — per-object transforms as unifo
         +rms means                                                      
      57 +      interpolating costs a lerp instead of a full CPU redraw. 
      58 +      The `TASKS.md` note that used to live here claimed the pa
         +inter's sort                                                   
      59 +      blocks this because it consumes `ContO.dist`. It does not
         +: `dist` is                                                    
      60 +      per-OBJECT (126 of them), not per-vertex, and can stay CP
         +U-side                                                         
      61 +      untouched while only the vertex transform moves. Still a 
         +large change —                                                 
      62 +      several hundred lines across `graphics.js` and `Plane.js`
         +.                                                              
      63 +- [ ] Cache polygon triangulation topology at load instead of r
         +e-deriving                                                     
      64 +      convexity and ear-clipping per frame. Blocked on the spli
         +t above; may be                                                
      65 +      worth nothing.

         +lay. Dropped.

● Update(WORK.md)
  ⎿  Added 2 lines
      29  - **Draw splits ~50/50 between projection and the batcher** (8 
          cars, res=2, `?raster=0` isolates it): 24.09ms normal vs 12.53m
          s with the emit calls no-op'd. So per-vertex projection in `Pla
          ne.d` is 12.5ms and triangulation + vertex-buffer writes in `gr
          aphics.js` are 11.6ms. Moving projection alone to a vertex shad
          er is worth ~2x, not 4x.
      30  - **Timing `GameSparker.tick()` as a unit reports `draw 0.00ms`
          .** `tick()` is `draw()` then `simulate()`, so with interpolati
          on off the entire draw cost lands in the "sim" number. Time the
           two halves separately — this is what produced the bogus 10.3/3
          .1 split above.
      31  - **With `interp=0` the game draws only on a tick, so fps is pi
          nned at 18.9 by construction.** Comparing render settings by fp
          s in that mode always reads flat; use the ms figures. Interpola
          tion is the only route past 18.9fps and each interpolated frame
           costs a full CPU redraw (~17ms).
      32 +- **`Math.imul` on a float operand silently zeroes it.** `Mad.c
         +olide`'s narrow-phase threshold is `(n3 + n4) * (comprad[a] + c
         +omprad[b])`; `comprad` is a `float[]` (formula7 = 0.4), so the 
         +port's `Math.imul` truncated the sum to 0 and the threshold bec
         +ame 0 — **no car in the default field ever collided**. Use `Mat
         +h.imul` ONLY when both operands are `int` in the Java; check th
         +e field's declared type, don't infer it from the values. Audite
         +d: `CarDefine.powerloss` was the only other candidate and is ge
         +nuinely `int[]`.                                               
      33 +- **Two identical cars never collide, and that's authentic.** `
         +colide()` picks a dominant car by `|power * speed * moment|`, t
         +hen by `moment`; on an exact tie neither dominates and no conta
         +ct is evaluated. Any collision test must make the two cars asym
         +metric or it silently tests nothing.                           
      34  - **Headless Chromium won't run rAF without `--screenshot`.** `
          --headless=new --virtual-time-budget=N --dump-dom` boots the pa
          ge, runs GL, and never fires a single animation frame, so the g
          ame appears hung. Adding `--screenshot=x.png --window-size=800,
          450` drives the compositor and it runs. This is the cheapest wa
          y to verify a browser change end-to-end (`--enable-logging=stde
          rr --vmodule=console=1` to read `console.log`).

● Three things done, nothing committed.

  web/ deleted (3 tracked files staged for removal, plus the two gitignored
  jars).

  PORT_SPEC — you're right, and I had it wrong. It isn't just historical: its
  "Using a subagent" and "Calibrate before batching" sections are live
  methodology — one representative class first, catalogue every systematic error
  into the template, only then fan out, and restate the painter's-algorithm
  constraint in every rendering prompt. AGENTS.md and TASKS.md now point at it
  for exactly that, instead of calling it superseded.

  The collision bug is fixed. js/Mad.js:2122:

  < Math.imul(n3 + n4, this.cd.comprad[mad.cn] + this.cd.comprad[this.cn])

  comprad is a float[] — formula7 is 0.4. Math.imul truncates both operands to
  int32, so 0.4 + 0.4 = 0.8 became 0, the narrow-phase threshold became 0, and
  rpy(...) < 0 is never true. No car in the default field ever collided. In the
  Java this is int * float and stays float. Fixed to fr(i32(n3 + n4) * 
  fr(comprad + comprad)).

  Verified by driving one car into a parked one, with and without the fix:

  ┌────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │        │ parked car displacement │ its speed │
  ├────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ before │ 0.0                     │ 0.00      │
  ├────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ after  │ 7941                    │ 363       │
  └────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────┘

  Two things worth carrying forward, both now in WORK.md:

  - I audited every other Math.imul in the port against the Java field
  declarations. CarDefine.powerloss was the only other candidate and is
  genuinely int[], so this was the single instance — but the rule is to check
  the declared type, never infer it from the values.
  - My first test showed no collision even after the fix, and that was the
  test's fault: colide() picks a dominant car by |power * speed * moment|, and
  two identical cars tie, so neither dominates and no contact is evaluated.
  That's authentic Java behaviour. Any collision test has to make the cars
  asymmetric or it silently tests nothing.

  Added a regression test for it — 100 tests pass. Deployed as 85e17c2d so you
  can drive into someone.

● No — 84 of the 86 are byte-identical extractions of data/models.zip, so
  committing them would duplicate ~850 KB of content the repo already has and
  create two sources of truth for the same models.

  The other two aren't game data at all:

  - badstat_car.rad — a stat(invalid) fixture
  - custom_formula7.rad — a well-formed custom car

  Both are referenced by js/CarDefine.test.js (lines 244, 304), but the test
  inlines its own copies rather than reading these files, so nothing depends on
  them on disk. They look like scratch files from the loadcar un-stubbing work.

  The two .rad files that are tracked (Example, MAX Revenge.rad, Simple Car.rad)
  came with the game in the initial commit — those are correct to keep.

  My suggestion: gitignore the extractions and let the two fixtures either be
  deleted or moved somewhere honest:

  mycars/*.rad
  !mycars/Example, MAX Revenge.rad
  !mycars/Simple Car.rad

  That keeps the game's own files tracked and ignores anything extracted at
  runtime. If you'd rather the two test fixtures survive, js/tools/fixtures/ is
  the place for them — then the tests could read them instead of inlining, which
  would remove a copy-paste. Want me to do either?

● Done and deployed (6afa5aad). ~30 lines including the page section.

  ?geom=0 and ?overlay=0 now stub the two layers independently; ?raster=0 still
  means both, so the earlier measurements stay comparable.

  Round 3b on the page — four runs:

  ┌─────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │             │                  what it isolates                  │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ A normal    │ —                                                  │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B overlay=0 │ HUD gone; Canvas2D cost removed                    │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ C geom=0    │ blank scene; triangulation + vertex writes removed │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ D raster=0  │ blank; projection only                             │
  └─────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Then overlay = A−B, geometry = A−C, projection = D.

  Node already ran it and shows exactly the blind spot that motivated this:
  baseline 11.09 ms, overlay=0 11.04 ms (no change — nullContext2D had already
  no-op'd it), geom=0 8.22 ms. So node attributes 26% to geometry and cannot see
  the overlay at all. Your browser measured 48% for the two combined, and the
  gap between those two numbers is the overlay — but that's inference, which is
  what B settles directly.

  Worth noting before you run it: the HUD images landed since perf3.txt, so the
  overlay does strictly more work now than when the 11.5 ms was measured. If B
  comes back large, the fix is cheap and obvious — the HUD is static between
  ticks and is being fully re-rasterised every frame, so it can be cached to a
  bitmap and blitted, or simply not redrawn on non-tick frames.

  isn't physically possible. The experiment is confounded, and I can see the
  flaw:

  Vertex count is the only proxy for scene complexity, and stubbing geometry 
  sets it to 0. Run A shows 64,002 verts — but perf3.txt's A was 102,885 for the
  same config. So scene load varies by ~60% between runs depending on where the
  car is and what's on screen, which swamps the 3 ms differences I'm trying to
  measure. C and D report 0 verts, so there's no way to tell whether they were
  heavier or lighter scenes than A. Let me fix the instrument:

● Those four runs can't be read as they stand — B, C and D all show more draw
  time than A while doing strictly less work, which means the differences
  between runs are dominated by something other than the thing I was varying.

  The cause is scene weight. Run A reports 64,002 verts; perf3.txt's A, same
  config, reported 102,885. That's a 60% swing depending on where the car is and
  how much is on screen — far larger than the 3 ms I was trying to detect. And
  my instrument made it invisible in exactly the runs that needed it: stubbing
  geometry sets the emitted-vertex count to 0, so C and D reported a scene of
  zero and there was no way to tell whether they were heavier or lighter than A.

  Fixed and deployed (c07d4615):

  - The geometry stubs now count their input instead of being pure no-ops, so
  every run reports scene weight regardless of what's stubbed.
  - The benchmark reports ns/vert submitted, which is comparable across runs of
  different weight, alongside the raw submitted/emitted counts.

  BENCHMARK  3.0s window, 3s warmup discarded  --  PAUSED, press R to rerun

    289 ns/vert submitted   (64002 submitted, 64002 emitted)
    buffer 1600x900   res=2 textres=1 aa=0 interp=0 players=8 stage=1 pool=0

  Please rerun A–D and compare the ns/vert line. Easiest way to keep them
  honest: don't touch the keys in any of the four — the field still moves under
  AI, but the camera stays put and the scene stays comparable.

  One caveat I should have said last round: Medium.random() bottoms out in
  unseeded Math.random() (it's in WORK.md), so runs are never bit-identical even
  with a fixed seed and no input. Normalising per vertex is what makes them
  comparable, not the seed.

● Clean data this time — submitted counts are within 0.6% across all four runs,
  so they're genuinely comparable.

  ┌─────────────┬─────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │             │ ns/vert │       share of draw       │
  ├─────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────┤

  │ C geom=0    │ 2245    │ geometry = 528 ns (19%)   │
  ├─────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ D raster=0  │ 2244    │ remainder = 2244 ns (81%) │
  └─────────────┴─────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  C and D agreeing to 1 ns independently confirms the overlay is negligible —
  exactly what you'd expect once B showed it too.

  This supersedes perf3.txt. That run put the batcher at 48%; it's really ~19%.
  The old figure was measured with raster=0 reporting a scene of zero verts, so
  a lighter scene got credited as a saving. Node said 26%, and node was closer
  to right than my browser number was.

  So: the overlay is nothing, triangulation and vertex writes are a fifth, and
  81% is the projection/traversal path.

  One thing I don't want to gloss over, because it affects what you'd do next.
  The remaining 2244 ns/vert is not necessarily per-vertex work — ContO.d() also
  does per-object culling, shadow setup and distance, and Plane.d() does
  per-face lighting and LOD. And the absolute numbers don't fit a per-vertex
  model across sessions: 40,512 emitted verts took 28.63 ms here, while
  perf2.txt had 100,254 verts taking 24.38 ms. Vertex count alone clearly
  doesn't predict cost, which means the "~0.25 µs/vertex" line in WORK.md is
  unreliable and I'll correct it.

  That matters because a vertex shader only helps the genuinely per-vertex part.
  If most of the 81% is per-object traversal, the big rewrite buys much less
  than 3x.

  Cheapest way to settle it, and the last measurement I'd ask for: add per-frame
  counters for objects and faces drawn alongside verts, then run once at
  players=1 and once at players=8. Two points against three variables tells us
  whether cost scales with objects or with vertices, and that decides whether
  the GPU work is worth its cost. It's ~10 lines and two runs. Want that, or
  would you rather I just start the projection rewrite and accept the risk?

● The counters answer it. Scene shape reproduces in node to within 0.1% of your
  browser (faces 1956 and 3261 exactly), so I got the 1-car projection count
  locally rather than asking for another run.

  Cost model, from your two runs:

  draw ≈ 9.8 ms fixed  +  1.01 µs × projected vertices

  The key distinction the earlier rounds missed: projected ≠ submitted.
  Plane.d() transforms 12–20 vertices per face (the master===1 LOD switch)
  before culling can decide whether to submit any. 14,799 vertices get projected
  to submit 10,263 — so 31% of projection work is thrown away, and
  per-submitted-vertex figures were never going to be consistent.

  Layer split, now normalised properly: overlay 1.8%, batcher 19%,
  projection/traversal 81%. This supersedes perf3.txt's ~50/50, which credited a
  lighter scene as a saving.

  Two things I can't stand behind, and won't pretend otherwise:

  - The 9.8 ms fixed term is unexplained. Node shows only 1.4 ms fixed on the
  same scene, and medium.d() — the obvious suspect — is just 1.8 ms / 16%.
  Something in the browser costs ~8 ms/frame that I haven't located.
  - Nothing counts the even-odd scanline fill, whose cost is proportional to
  polygon area, not vertices. Checkpoint glyphs on screen in one run and not the
  other would move the fixed term with no counter changing. That's a plausible
  explanation for both the unexplained term and the earlier session-to-session
  inconsistency.

  Unrelated but worth having: ?pool=1 is a 30% pessimization (10.96 → 14.27 ms,
  node, identical scene). WORK.md recorded it as a 1.13x speedup, and your
  in-game A/B "showed no difference" because it compared fps — pinned at 18.9.
  It's off by default; now recorded as harmful rather than neutral.

  My recommendation: projection is the only term that provably scales and it's
  the majority, so GPU projection is still the right call — but budget it as
  ~2–3x on draw, not 5x, because that fixed term won't move. If you want one
  more cheap thing first, chasing the 9.8 ms is likely a smaller change than the
  shader rewrite and might turn out to be the same size of win. Your call —
  deployed as 44573ae8, nothing committed.

● Deployed (88a87cfd). Two corrections that came out of chasing this:

  The overlay was never a suspect. gs.draw() makes zero 2D-context calls — 1870
  fillPolygon, 193 drawPolygon, 2063 setColor, nothing else. xt.stat() runs from
  simulate(), not draw(). So ?overlay=0's 1.8% was noise, and the layer table
  should read: batcher ~19%, projection/traversal ~81%, overlay not in the draw
  path at all.

  The 9.8 ms may not exist. Given your machine/Firefox note, the node comparison
  proves nothing — different CPU, and SpiderMonkey vs V8 differ a lot on
  exactly this kind of Math.fround-heavy float32 code. The only real evidence
  was a two-point fit at 8,648 and 14,799 projected verts, which is poor
  leverage: a few percent of noise moves the intercept by milliseconds. Node's
  within-run regression over 1800 frames gives slope 0.988 µs and intercept 0.03
  ms — and that slope matches your marginal 1.01 µs almost exactly, which
  suggests the slope is the whole story and the intercept was fitting noise.

  So the benchmark now does that regression in your browser: one point per
  frame, hundreds per run, printed as

    fit over 187 frames: 1.013 us/projected vert + 0.24 ms fixed   (avg 12440
  projected)

  One run at ?bench=10, and drive around during it — the wider the spread of
  scene weights, the tighter the fit. There's a second link with geom=0 if the
  first result is ambiguous.

  One caveat for Firefox specifically: privacy.reduceTimerPrecision clamps
  performance.now() (1 ms by default on ESR). At ~20 ms per frame that's ~5%
  noise per point, which 190 points absorbs fine — but it's why I'd rather have
  10 s than 3.

● Deployed (4c0bff26). The regression approach failed, so I've switched to
  measuring the thing directly.

  Why B invalidates round 4: a negative fixed cost (−3.44 ms) is impossible, and
  B's slope (1.687 µs) came out higher than A's (1.083 µs) despite doing
  strictly less work. Both symptoms of the same thing — slope and intercept are
  collinear because scene weight varies too little within a run, so the fit
  distributes the total between them arbitrarily. A's 7.68 ms is the same
  artifact with a plausible-looking sign. I should have reported the x-range and
  R² from the start; the fit line now does, so a meaningless fit is visible as
  one.

  What the two runs do establish, from the totals rather than the fit: geometry
  batching is 18.59 − 16.09 = 2.5 ms, ~13% of draw. That's consistent with the
  19% from round 3b and with node's 26%, and it's measured, not extrapolated.

  Round 5 replaces the inference with a stopwatch: ?prof=1 times medium.d() —
  the sky, ground and terrain backdrop — separately from the object loop. That's
  the natural candidate for a per-frame cost that scales with neither cars nor
  vertices, and in node it's 1.8 ms / 16% of draw. One run, drive around as
  before.

  - backdrop several ms → that's the fixed cost, and it's a contained target.
  - backdrop under a millisecond → there's no fixed cost worth chasing, round
  4's 7.68 ms was fitting noise, and projection is the whole story.

  Either answer moves us to the GPU decision, which is where I'd stop measuring
  regardless — we've had four rounds and the marginal information per round is
  dropping.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Now I have the shape of it. Note the scanline path emits 55% of all vertices 
  in the frame — but ?geom=0 showed the entire batcher is only 2.5 ms, so those
  vertices aren't the cost. The backdrop's 6.38 ms is its own computation. Let

  Ran 5 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Found it. medium.d() — the backdrop — is 6.38 ms/frame, 34% of draw, and it's
  real: the direct measurement corroborates the 8.94 ms intercept, which now has
  a proper x-range (1461–14368) behind it.

  Breaking the backdrop down (node, so treat as proportions):

  ┌────────────────────┬──────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │                    │  ms  │          what it is           │
  ├────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ sky + ground bands │ 0.97 │ 36 full-screen gradient quads │
  ├────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ clouds             │ 0.80 │                               │
  ├────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ mountains          │ 0.44 │                               │
  ├────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ total              │ 2.21 │ ×~3 on your machine → ~6.4 ms │
  └────────────────────┴──────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  No single hotspot — it's spread across all three. Two things worth knowing
  that came out of the dig:

  The even-odd scanline fill emits 55% of every frame's vertices — 271 polygons
  out of ~2000 produce 22,110 of 40,062 vertices. It emits one quad per scanline
  row, so a 6-gon covering 60 pixels becomes ~60 quads. But ?geom=0 proves the
  whole batcher is only 2.5 ms of CPU, so this is not a CPU problem — it's
  vertex-processing and overdraw on the GPU, invisible to these timers. It's a
  plausible part of why you found lower resolution noticeably faster back in
  round 1.

  Some of those are genuinely self-intersecting. Backdrop quads arrive in TL, 
  TR, BL, BR order — bowties — so even-odd is correct and a triangle fan would
  be wrong. A bowtie's fill is exactly two triangles meeting at the edge
  crossing, i.e. 6 vertices rather than the 90–108 currently emitted, and that's
  a contained fix for the n=4 cases (45 polys/frame). The n=6 and n=8 cases
  (164 polys, 13,200 verts) would need a real trapezoidal decomposition — O(n)
  trapezoids instead of O(height) scanline quads, ~12× fewer vertices.

  Where that leaves the three candidates:

  - Backdrop, 6.4 ms / 34% — no single hotspot, so it needs either fewer
  gradient bands (a visual change) or moving the gradient to a fragment shader.
  Medium effort, and the visual risk is on you.
  - Trapezoidal fill — removes ~55% of emitted vertices for zero CPU win but
  real GPU relief. Contained to graphics.js, maybe 80 lines, and I'd want a
  pixel diff to confirm it doesn't change the look.
  - GPU projection, ~12.5 ms / 66% — still the biggest, still the largest
  change.

  I'd stop measuring here; the marginal information per round has clearly
  dropped. My recommendation is the trapezoidal fill next — it's the smallest of
  the three, it's testable against a pixel diff, and it directly targets the
  resolution sensitivity you noticed at the very start. Want that, or the
  backdrop, or straight to projection?

● 36% fewer vertices (40,041 → 25,455) and 8.4% faster CPU. Now the visual check
  — this is the part that matters, since the fill is geometrically exact rather

  Read 4 files, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Visually identical at 3× zoom; the 1.4% differing pixels are sub-pixel edges
  on cloud and mountain boundaries. Let me lock the correctness in with a test:

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Trapezoid fill is in and deployed (9f52212b).

  Result: 40,041 → 25,455 vertices/frame (−36%), 8.4% faster CPU in node.

  The old fill emitted one quad per pixel row, so cost scaled with polygon
  height. The new one cuts at every vertex y and every edge-edge crossing,
  emitting one trapezoid per span per band — O(vertices) instead of O(height).
  Per-polygon on synthetic cases: bowtie quad 222 → 18 verts, concave arrow 360
  → 18, keyhole glyph 366 → 30.

  Correctness is checked against a ground-truth even-odd point-in-polygon test,
  not against the old implementation — comparing the two would only prove they
  agree, not that either is right. All three shapes come back pixel-exact. Four
  new tests, 104 passing.

  The intersection-y events are load-bearing, and it's worth knowing why:
  backdrop quads arrive ordered TL, TR, BL, BR, which is self-intersecting.
  java.awt fills those as an hourglass. If a crossing isn't a band boundary, the
  left-to-right span pairing inside that band is wrong — and a triangle fan
  would fill the whole bounding quad instead, which is why the slow path existed
  in the first place.

  Visually identical: 1.4% of pixels differ, all sub-pixel edges on cloud and
  mountain silhouettes, because trapezoids are exact in continuous space where
  the scanline version snapped to pixel rows. Side-by-side at 3× zoom shows no
  perceptible difference.

  Two things I'd flag:

  - I told you "~12×" when proposing this, then corrected to "2–3×" once I saw
  the polygons are only 4–26 rows tall. The measured 36% overall is between
  those, because tall polygons benefit most and there are a few.
  - This is CPU-side only. It doesn't change fill area at all, so it won't touch
  the GPU overdraw that made resolution matter to you — it reduces vertex
  processing and ~175 KB/frame of upload.

  Round 6 on the test page has an A/B against ?fill=scan if you want to confirm
  the win on Firefox, where the JIT differs. Nothing committed.

      193 -    <h2>Settled — no need to retest</h2>                      
      194 -    <p><strong>Physics is not the bottleneck.</strong> 0.6–1.7
          - ms/tick. The 10.3 ms figure in WORK.md was sim+draw measured 
          -as one.</p>                                                   
      195 -    <p><strong>Resolution is nearly free on the CPU</strong> (
          -res=1 24.1 ms vs res=2 24.1 ms); MSAA at 2x cost ~12% and is n
          -ow off above 1x. GPU fill time is invisible to these timers, s
          -o this is a CPU statement only.</p>                           
      196 -    <p><strong>fps is pinned to 18.9</strong> whenever interpo
          -lation is off — the game draws only on a tick. Don't compare b
          -uilds by fps in that mode.</p>                                
      197 -    <p><strong>Interpolation</strong> — the only route past 18
          -.9 fps, but the car jitters and each interpolated frame costs 
          -a full ~17 ms CPU redraw. Off by default.</p>                 
      198 -    <p><strong>Array pooling</strong> — no measurable differen
          -ce. Dropped.</p>

          -aches 60.</p>

      154 +  <details>                                                   
      155 +    <summary>What's been measured — and what turned out to be 
          +wrong</summary>                                               
      156 +    <p><strong>Physics is not the bottleneck</strong> — 0.6–2.
          +5 ms/tick. Drawing is 15–25 ms.</p>                           
      157 +    <p><strong>Draw splits</strong> roughly: projection and tr
          +aversal ~80%, geometry batching ~13%, Canvas2D overlay ~0 — th
          +e HUD is drawn from <code>simulate()</code>, not <code>draw()<
          +/code>.</p>                                                   
      158 +    <p><strong>Cost tracks PROJECTED vertices</strong> (~1.0–1
          +.3 µs each), not submitted ones: <code>Plane.d</code> transfor
          +ms 12–20 vertices per face before culling decides whether to s
          +ubmit any.</p>                                                
      159 +    <p><strong>The trapezoid fill</strong> replaced a per-pixe
          +l-row scanline fill — emitted verts 57,069 → 37,269, backdrop 
          +6.08 → 3.83 ms.</p>                                           
      160 +    <p><strong>fps cannot measure rendering</strong> with inte
          +rpolation off: the game draws only on a tick, so it is pinned 
          +at 18.9 by construction. Use the ms figures.</p>              
      161 +    <p><strong>Array pooling is 30% slower</strong>, not the 1
          +.13× faster once recorded.</p>                                
      162 +    <p><strong>Resolution is not free</strong> — vertex work i
          +s, fragment bandwidth is not; with no depth buffer the whole s
          +cene overdraws.</p>

      165 +                                                              
      166 +<script type="module">                                        
      167 +  const form = document.getElementById('f');                  
      168 +  const urlBox = document.getElementById('url');              
      169 +  const fields = [...form.querySelectorAll('input, select')]; 
      170 +                                                              
      171 +  // Stubs read `=0`, so their box being TICKED means the para
          +meter appears as                                              
      172 +  // 0. Everything else appears as 1 when ticked.             
      173 +  const INVERTED = new Set(['geom', 'overlay', 'raster']);    
      174 +                                                              
      175 +  // `aa` has no fixed default -- it follows res (on at 1x, of
          +f above), so it                                               
      176 +  // is emitted only when the box disagrees with what res woul
          +d have picked.                                                
      177 +  const defaultAA = () => parseFloat(document.getElementById('
          +res').value || '2') <= 1;                                     
      178 +                                                              
      179 +  function build() {                                          
      180 +    const params = new URLSearchParams();                     
      181 +    for (const el of fields) {                                
      182 +      const id = el.id;                                       
      183 +      let changed = false;                                    
      184 +      if (el.type === 'checkbox') {                           
      185 +        if (id === 'aa') {                                    
      186 +          changed = el.checked !== defaultAA();               
      187 +          if (changed) params.set('aa', el.checked ? '1' : '0'
          +);                                                            
      188 +        } else if (INVERTED.has(id)) {                        
      189 +          changed = el.checked;                               
      190 +          if (changed) params.set(id, '0');                   
      191 +        } else {                                              
      192 +          changed = el.checked;                               
      193 +          if (changed) params.set(id, '1');                   
      194 +        }                                                     
      195 +      } else {                                                
      196 +        const def = el.dataset.def ?? '';                     
      197 +        const val = el.value.trim();                          
      198 +        changed = val !== '' && val !== def;                  
      199 +        if (changed) params.set(id, val);                     
      200 +      }                                                       
      201 +      const row = el.closest('.row') || el.closest('.check'); 
      202 +      if (row) row.classList.toggle('changed', changed);      
      203 +    }                                                         
      204 +    const qs = params.toString();                             
      205 +    const url = 'main.html' + (qs ? '?' + qs : '');           
      206 +    urlBox.textContent = url;                                 
      207 +    return url;                                               
      208 +  }                                                           
      209 +                                                              
      210 +  function reset() {                                          
      211 +    for (const el of fields) {                                
      212 +      if (el.type === 'checkbox') el.checked = false;         
      213 +      else el.value = el.dataset.def ?? '';                   
      214 +    }                                                         
      215 +    document.getElementById('aa').checked = defaultAA();      
      216 +    build();                                                  
      217 +  }                                                           
      218 +                                                              
      219 +  // Restore from this page's own query string, so a configura
          +tion can be                                                   
      220 +  // bookmarked and Back from a run returns you to what you la
          +unched.                                                       
      221 +  reset();                                                    
      222 +  for (const [k, v] of new URLSearchParams(location.search)) {
      223 +    const el = document.getElementById(k);                    
      224 +    if (!el) continue;                                        
      225 +    if (el.type === 'checkbox') el.checked = INVERTED.has(k) ?
          + v === '0' : v === '1';                                       
      226 +    else el.value = v;                                        
      227 +  }                                                           
      228 +  build();                                                    
      229 +                                                              
      230 +  form.addEventListener('input', build);                      
      231 +  form.addEventListener('submit', (e) => { e.preventDefault();
          + location.href = build(); });                                 
      232 +  document.getElementById('newtab').onclick = () => window.ope
          +n(build(), '_blank');                                         
      233 +  document.getElementById('reset').onclick = reset;           
      234 +  document.getElementById('copy').onclick = async (e) => {    
      235 +    await navigator.clipboard.writeText(new URL(build(), locat
          +ion.href).href);                                              
      236 +    const btn = e.target;                                     
      237 +    btn.textContent = 'Copied';                               
      238 +    setTimeout(() => { btn.textContent = 'Copy URL'; }, 1200);
      239 +  };                                                          
      240 +</script>

● Both done and deployed (89ef4dc2).

  Settings GUI — six fieldsets at the site root, live URL preview that omits
  anything at its default, changed rows bolded, Launch / new tab / reset / copy.
  It reads its own query string back into the form, so a config is bookmarkable
  and Back from a run returns you to what you launched.

  Stage-specific opponents — ported xtGraphics.sortcars() (~180 lines). You were
  right that it's stage-driven, and it doesn't need the menu code at all: it's
  self-contained, so it patches in cleanly now.

  It draws slots 1–6 by rejection sampling — (15 - car) / 15 * (stage / 10),
  capped at 0.8 — so slow cars get rejected more often the later the stage and
  the grid drifts up through the roster. Then specific stages force specific
  opponents. Stage 1 now fields Tornado Shark, Nimi, Drifter X, M.A.S.H.E.E.N.,
  La Vita Crab and MAX Revenge; stage 10 forces in a Radical One and an EL KING.

  Three details worth recording:

  - Stages above 10 do n -= 10 early, so the later if (n == 12) tests compare
  against the reduced value. Stage 12 therefore doesn't get the stage-12 forced
  car — stage 22 does. That reads like a bug in the original; I've kept it
  as-is.
  - n7 is reassigned between picking the car and weighting the rejection, so the
  two uses genuinely differ for bonus stages. That looks like a decompiler
  artifact and isn't.
  - Slots 1–6 only — the original grid is seven cars. With ?players=8 the eighth
  slot gets your car.

  ?cars=same restores the old all-identical behaviour, and it's in the GUI as an
  "Opponents" dropdown. 104 tests pass. Nothing committed since 24ac353.

      68 + * Four comparable bars per car, taken from the physics tables

      69 + * already holds rather than from any menu artwork:            
      70 + *                                                             
      71 + *   Speed     swits[car][2]  the top gear's switch point      
      72 + *   Accel     acelf[car][0]  first-gear acceleration

      79  export function carStats(car) {

      70 -  const pct = (v) => Math.max(0, Math.min(100, Math.round(v)));
