      81 +  const best = (pick) => {                                     
      82 +    let hi = 0;                                                
      83 +    for (let i = 0; i < CAR_COUNT; i++) hi = Math.max(hi, pick(
         +i));                                                           
      84 +    return hi || 1;                                            
      85 +  };                                                           
      86 +  const bar = (pick) => Math.round((pick(car) / best(pick)) * 1
         +00);                                                           
      87 +  return {                                                     
      88 +    cls: CLASSES[cd.cclass[car]] ?? '',                        
      89 +    bars: [                                                    
      90 +      ['Speed', bar((i) => cd.swits[i][2])],                   
      91 +      ['Accel', bar((i) => cd.acelf[i][0])],                   
      92 +      ['Grip', bar((i) => cd.grip[i])],                        
      93 +      ['Strength', bar((i) => cd.maxmag[i])],                  
      94 +    ],                                                         
      95 +  };

● Deployed (9d2f7467).

  Yes — both were already there, and using them was the right call. Two separate
  setups in xtGraphics.java, and I've taken the best of each:

  - Car-maker preview (:6806) — the rotating one. xz += 5 per frame, wzy -= 10
  so the wheels turn with the body, car parked at z=1000, and it draws the base 
  model directly rather than wrapping it in a new ContO. That's where the spin
  comes from.
  - Car-select screen (:5058) — camera at (-400, -525, -50), zy=10, ground=495.
  I switched to this one because the car-maker's camera is level with the car
  and shows its underside, which looks wrong when the car is the subject.

  Copying the constants exactly is what fixed the earlier tiny, unlit render:
  focus_point, cx, cy are absolute screen coordinates, so the canvas has to be
  800×450 game space and the page crops with CSS instead of moving the camera.
  Backing store is 2× so the 4× crop stays sharp.

  Also fixed:

  - Ground is transparent now. hpground tiles were burying the route in a
  uniform grid. This needed the base-model index on each placed object, so
  loadbase tags the base models and ContO's copy constructor carries it — two
  lines, clearly marked as non-game state.
  - Stat bars work — they were <span>s, so height was doing nothing until
  display:block. Formula 7 now reads correctly: max speed, max accel, high grip,
  9% strength.

  The radar (radarstat) is ported too, but it's a car-centred HUD radar rather
  than a stage map, so the minimap stays as-is — and per your note it's
  structured to swap for a 3D rotate later.

  104 tests pass. Nothing committed since 24ac353; the launcher, preview.js,
  sortcars, and the baseIndex tag are all uncommitted.

● Both fixed, deployed (056391bb).

  The shadow wasn't the problem — the camera was. I'd mixed two setups: the
  car-maker's car position (z=1000, ground=2470) with the car-select camera. The
  car-maker camera sits level with the car, so you were seeing the underside
  and the ground plane edge-on behind it. Now it uses the car-select camera
  consistently (y=-525, zy=10, ground=495), which looks down at the car with the
  shadow beneath. Also zoomed out ~30% as asked.

  The minimap was mislabelling every object. loadbase places track pieces at
  slot 56 + j, not 16 + j — cars occupy 0–15 but there's a gap before the track
  models. My name table assumed they were contiguous, so hpground never matched
  and the ground filter silently did nothing; that's why it looked like uniform
  disjoint tiles. The names now come from GameSparker's own exported lists with
  the correct offset, so it can't drift again.

  With real names available, the map now distinguishes what matters: road solid,
  arena walls dim, scenery dimmer, checkpoints in yellow, start in cyan — and
  the road tiles are drawn at their true footprint (≈1.41 × the bounding radius)
  so they abut into a continuous path instead of dots. Stage 3's loop reads
  clearly now.

  The face is in — d1.png from images.zip, left of the title. It uses the game's
  own loadude keying rather than a plain chroma key: greenish pixels become
  black with alpha 255 - (g - (r+b)/2) × 1.5, so the antialiased fringe becomes
  a soft outline instead of a green halo. It's loaded non-blocking, so a missing
  images.zip costs you the decoration, not the launcher.

  Two small changes outside the launcher, both marked as non-game state:
  loadbase tags base models with their slot index, ContO's copy constructor
  carries it, and CAR_NAMES/TRACK_NAMES are now exported.

  104 tests pass. Still uncommitted since 24ac353.

  ⎿  Added 63 lines, removed 4 lines
       1 -# Need for Madness                                             
       1 +# Need for Madness                                             
       2  
       3 +A 2015 Java game, ported to run natively in the browser as Java
         +Script + WebGL.                                                
       4 +The original source was never released, so the port is written 
         +from decompiled                                                
       5 +bytecode and checked against the running jar.                  
       6 +                                                               
       7 +```sh                                                          
       8 +./start.sh                    # the original game, patched for 
         +modern Java                                                    
       9 +python3 -m http.server 8123   # then open http://localhost:8123
         +/                                                              
      10 +./deploy.sh                   # publish the web port to the sta
         +tic host                                                       
      11 +```                                                            
      12 +                                                               
      13  ## Architecture
      14  
       5 -- `web/` -                                                     
       6 -- `java/` -                                                    
       7 -- `decompilation/` -                                           
      15 +- **`web/`** — the JS/WebGL port. `main.html` is the game; the 
         +launcher at the                                                
      16 +  repo root points at it. Everything the browser loads lives he
         +re or in the                                                   
      17 +  shared data directories.                                     
      18 +- **`java/`** — the original game: the patched `Game.jar` that 
         +`start.sh` runs,                                               
      19 +  the pristine `Game.jar.bak`, and the previous build for rollb
         +ack. This is the                                               
      20 +  reference implementation the port is compared against, not a 
         +build input.                                                   
      21 +- **`decompilation/`** — the decompiled Java (`java-src/`), the
         + porting                                                       
      22 +  contract (`PORT_SPEC.md`), and the records of the delegated t
         +ranspilation                                                   
      23 +  jobs (`agy_tmp`, `agy_batch_tmp`). Read-only history and refe
         +rence.                                                         
      24 +- **`data/`, `stages/`, `mycars/`, `mystages/`, `music/`** — ga
         +me assets, byte                                                
      25 +  identical to the original and **not to be modified**. They st
         +ay at the root                                                 
      26 +  because both the jar and the port read them.                 
      27 +                                                               
      28 +### Why the data stays at the root                             
      29 +                                                               
      30 +`vfs.detectFpath()` probes `./` then `../` for `data/models.zip
         +`, so one build                                                
      31 +works from either depth: the launcher at `/index.html` resolves
         + `./data`, and                                                 
      32 +the game at `/web/main.html` resolves `../data`. `deploy.sh` mi
         +rrors that shape                                               
      33 +onto the host, which is why the deployed tree is not flattened.
      34 +                                                               
      35 +## How the port works                                          
      36 +                                                               
      37 +The renderer is the interesting part. There is **no depth buffe
         +r**: occlusion                                                 
      38 +comes entirely from submission order, so colour is a vertex att
         +ribute, the                                                    
      39 +whole frame is one draw call, and nothing may be batched, sorte
         +d or split by                                                  
      40 +primitive type. `web/graphics.js` opens with the full statement
         + of that                                                       
      41 +constraint; read it before changing anything there.            
      42 +                                                               
      43 +Physics runs at a fixed 18.9 Hz because every constant in `Mad.
         +drive()` is                                                    
      44 +per-tick. Drawing dominates the frame — physics is 0.6–2.5 ms a
         +gainst 15–25 ms                                                
      45 +of rendering — and the cost tracks *projected* vertices, of whi
         +ch `Plane.d`                                                   
      46 +transforms 12–20 per face before culling decides whether to sub
         +mit any.                                                       
      47 +                                                               
      48 +Transpiled classes follow `web/TRANSPILE_SPEC.md`: Java int ari
         +thmetic wraps at                                               
      49 +32 bits, `float` is not `double`, and both have to be reproduce
         +d explicitly or                                                
      50 +the simulation drifts rather than failing outright.            
      51 +                                                               
      52 +## Documentation                                               
      53 +                                                               
      54 +| file | what it is |                                          
      55 +| --- | --- |                                                  
      56 +| `AGENTS.md` | how to run, deploy, measure and verify; the inv
         +ariants |                                                      
      57 +| `WORK.md` | discoveries and gotchas, newest last — including 
         +several measurements that overturned earlier conclusions |     
      58 +| `TASKS.md` | what is done, what is next, what is blocked |   
      59 +| `web/TRANSPILE_SPEC.md` | the Java→JS contract |             
      60 +| `decompilation/PORT_SPEC.md` | the original plan, and the sti
         +ll-binding rules for delegating work |                         
      61 +                                                               
      62 +## Status                                                      
      63 +                                                               
      64 +Racing works: stage loading, physics, collisions, the in-race H
         +UD, and a                                                      
      65 +stage-appropriate opponent grid. Not yet ported: audio, the men
         +us and car/stage                                               
      66 +select screens (~9600 lines of `xtGraphics`), and the car/stage
         + makers.

● Both fixed and deployed (1f2bf6a1). You were right on both counts, and the
  second one turned up something useful.

  Shadow. Not the camera — I was wrong about that too. ContO.d's crs path
  projects a flat silhouette that assumes the car is sitting on a track surface;
  with it floating, the silhouette lands across the model's middle. I tried
  seating it properly (y = ground - grat, as loadstage does) and that made the
  shadow the entire picture. So the preview now just doesn't draw one — shadow
  off for the duration of the call, restored after. A product shot doesn't need
  a shadow, and this beats inventing a placement the game never uses.

  Stage preview is now real geometry, rendered by the game's renderer with the
  camera pointed straight down — actual track surfaces and shading, not coloured
  blocks.

  Getting there found a genuine discovery: at map scale, only 7 of 190 objects 
  survived culling. ContO.d's distance-fade and minimum-projected-size tests are
  both written as ... || this.m.trk !== 0, so at that zoom everything vanishes
  without it. m.trk = 2 is the StageMaker's overhead editing mode
  (StageMaker.java:886) and exists precisely for this. trk = 1 would also hide
  decor; 2 keeps it.

  The camera height is derived from the projection rather than guessed — at
  zy=90 the transform collapses to screenX = cx + dx·focus/depth, so fitting the
  stage needs depth ≥ max(spanX/2, spanZ·400/450).

  That's another case of the original already having what we needed, like the
  car-select camera and sortcars. I'll add both trk and the shadow finding to
  WORK.md — worth recording that overhead rendering has a mode flag, since it's
  invisible from the JS side.

  104 tests pass. The reorg plus all of this is still uncommitted.

● Using the game's own extent (cleaner, and no longer skewed by outliers):

  exceeds the depth limit — both fall back. Deploying:

● Deployed (99194474). Your instinct was right and it gave the clean answer:

  maxl/maxr/maxt/maxb in the stage file are the four boundary walls, and 
  loadstage passes exactly those to trackers.devidetrackers(). So after a load,
  trackers.sx / sz / ncx*3000 / ncz*3000 is the game's own bounding box. I was
  inferring one from object positions, which is both wrong (a single outlying
  object drags the centre) and unnecessary. Now it uses the real extent.

  The preview race is fixed — that was your main report. Two causes, both in one
  function: a metadata cache hit returned early without rebuilding the stage,
  so the camera moved to the new stage's bounds while placed still held the old
  geometry (hence "stuck but translating around"); and nothing serialised access
  to the single shared world, so spam-clicking interleaved loadstage calls and
  corrupted it into stage === -3. It now always rebuilds and runs through a
  promise queue.

  Stage 8 I could not fix. With correct bounds it emits 102 vertices from 172
  objects that pass every gate I could verify by hand; the loss is inside
  Plane.d's face culling at ~91k depth and I don't understand it. Stage 20 needs
  more depth than anything that renders. Both now fall back to the flat map
  rather than showing black — that's a workaround, not a fix, and it's logged in
  TASKS.md as unresolved.

  The stage 9 smear I haven't reproduced since the camera-clearance change, so
  I've listed it as unconfirmed rather than claiming it fixed.

  Also done: path added to the advanced panel (it already held every other
  parameter), and WORK.md/TASKS.md updated with the six findings from this
  stretch — the loadbase 56-offset, m.trk as the thing that makes overhead views
  possible, the trackers extent, the crs shadow assumption, the canvas
  one-context-for-life rule, and the overhead-view limit.

  I burned a lot of turns on the stage-8 dead end. 104 tests pass; still
  uncommitted since 24ac353.

● That's a real bug and a cheap fix. rd.begin() clears the 2D overlay, and the
  interpolated redraw calls it after simulate() has drawn the HUD there:

  Read 1 file, ran 4 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)
● Fixed and deployed (1c614ecc) — HUD now identical with interp=0 and interp=1.

  Worth fixing, and cheap: three lines. The cause is a structural detail worth
  remembering — the HUD is drawn from simulate(), not draw(), so it lands on the
  2D overlay once per tick. rd.begin() clears that overlay, and the
  interpolated path called it after simulate() had already painted the HUD,
  wiping it every interpolated frame. begin() now takes keepOverlay, which
  interpolated redraws pass — the overlay then holds the last tick's HUD, which
  is exactly the rate it updates at anyway.

  That's the same fact that explained the earlier ?overlay=0 result reading as
  noise: gs.draw() makes zero 2D-context calls.

● Let me check two things before answering, since both are checkable rather than
  guessable.

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed the launcher lets you pick a camera mode without interpolation on.
  But rather than theorise about the blend case again, let me measure it:

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Headless can only manage 3 fps, so I can't reproduce the blend case — but the
  counter does increment in both modes there, so it isn't frozen by an
  exception. Here's what I can tell you from the code.

  The 19 fps default is by construction, not a bug. With interp=0 the game draws
  only when a tick runs, so the render rate is the tick rate. Interpolation is
  the only route past it.

  The re-derive jitter has a specific cause. medium.follow() is a stateful 
  easing step — it nudges the camera a fraction of the way toward the car each
  call, it's not a function of the interpolation fraction. Each interpolated
  frame does: restoreCurr() (camera back to the tick state) → follow() once. So
  within a tick the camera only ever sits one ease step from where the tick left
  it, while the car travels a whole tick's worth. At each tick boundary the
  camera snaps to catch up. That per-tick discontinuity at 19 Hz is what you're
  seeing, and it explains your original observation that the car jitters rather
  than the camera — the car is moving smoothly against a background that lurches
  once per tick.

  So cam=blend is the theoretically right mode, and the comment in the code
  arguing against it is wrong: it warns that blending camera and car
  independently makes them disagree mid-turn, but both are interpolated over the
  same t between the same tick endpoints, so they stay consistent by
  construction.

  Which makes your blend result the important anomaly, and I need one number to
  chase it — the counter can't be "locked" to the tick rate in that path, since
  with interpolation on every rAF counts a frame regardless of camera mode.

  What I'd do, in order:

  1. Get the real reading — run interp=1&cam=blend&stats=1&bench=3 and paste the
  block. If fps avg really is ~18.9 with draw well under 50 ms, something in
  the blend path is silently killing frames and that's the first fix. If it's
  ~30 but looks like 19, the camera is being blended to a constant and it's a
  state-capture bug.
  2. Fix re-derive or drop it — as written it can't do better than a per-tick
  snap.
  3. UI: the camera control is selectable with interpolation off, where it does
  nothing. I'll gate it.
  4. Then GPU projection — each interpolated frame currently costs a full ~18 ms
  CPU redraw, which is the ceiling on how high fps can go even once the jitter
  is fixed.

  Want me to do 3 now and take 1 when you've got the number?

● That settles it: blend renders at 58.3 fps — the counter isn't locked at all.
  So the "19 fps in blend" run almost certainly had interpolation off, which the
  launcher makes easy: the Camera control is selectable and does nothing unless
  interp=1. Let me close that trap:

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Deployed (654df24f). The Camera control is now greyed out and disabled unless
  interpolation is ticked, with the hint changing to explain what each mode does
  once it's live.

  Your blend run is actually the good news: 58.3 fps at 19.0 tick/s, so
  interpolation is working and the render rate is triple the tick rate. The
  counter was never capable of locking to 19 in that path — every rAF counts a
  frame when interpolation is on. The earlier reading was almost certainly a URL
  with the camera set but interp unticked.

  So the state of play is:

  - cam=blend — 58 fps, and theoretically the correct mode. Both the camera and
  the cars are interpolated over the same t between the same tick endpoints, so
  they stay consistent by construction. The code comment arguing against it is
  wrong.
  - cam=derive — structurally can't be smooth. follow() is a stateful easing
  step, not a function of t; each frame restores the camera to the tick state
  and applies exactly one ease step, so the camera lurches once per tick while
  the cars move continuously. That's your jitter.

  What I'd do next, and I'd like your read before changing behaviour: make blend
  the default and delete derive, since it can't be fixed without rewriting
  follow() into a pure function of the interpolation fraction. Worth you driving
  a lap on ?interp=1&cam=blend first — if that looks smooth, the jitter item in
  TASKS.md closes and interpolation becomes the default, which lifts the game
  off 19 fps for good.

  The ceiling after that is cost, not correctness: 13.07 ms/frame of draw at 79%
  of a core means ~60 fps is roughly the limit, and GPU projection is what
  would give real headroom.

❯ lets try to fix the issue first.  ?interp=1&cam=blend is jittery              

● Let me check something specific — how rotation is evaluated, because that

❯ jittery on turns specifically                                                 

● That's the answer — and it explains why turns specifically. Rotation is a
  360-entry lookup table indexed by whole degrees, so a blended heading gets
  rounded to 1° steps. Position interpolates smoothly, heading snaps; on a
  straight there's nothing to snap, on a turn it snaps constantly.

  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Now stop rounding the blended angles, since fractional headings are finally
  expressible:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Tests still pass — which is the important safety check: integer angles take
  the identical branch, so the simulation is untouched and the differential
  tests confirm it. Let me verify rendering:
  Read 1 file, ran 5 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Deployed (35b31d54). "Jittery on turns specifically" was the clue that pinned
  it.

  Medium.sin/cos are 360-entry tables indexed by whole degrees. A blended
  heading therefore had to be rounded to an integer degree — tsin[1.5] would
  have returned undefined, which is exactly why the rounding was there. At
  ~800px across a ~60° view, one degree is ~13 pixels of yaw. So interpolation
  translated smoothly but rotated in 13px jumps. On a straight the heading is
  constant and there's nothing to snap; on a turn it snaps every frame. That's
  your symptom precisely, and it applies to both camera and car headings, which
  is why neither derive nor blend escaped it.

  The fix is small: both functions now lerp between neighbouring table entries
  when given a fractional angle, and the blend no longer rounds xz/xy/zy.
  Positions stay rounded — they're integers in the game and a unit is far below
  a pixel.

  The simulation is untouched. An integer argument hits if (i0 === i) return 
  this.tsin[i0] — the identical path, the identical float32. That's not just an
  assertion: the 104 tests include the differential ones that would catch any
  drift, and they pass.

  Please drive a lap on ?interp=1&cam=blend and take a corner.

  Two things that follow from this:

  - If it's smooth now, I'd make blend the default and delete derive. derive is
  structurally unfixable — follow() is a stateful ease rather than a function of
  the interpolation fraction, so it lurches once per tick no matter what.
  - Interpolation then becomes the default and the game is off 19 fps for good.
  Your own measurement puts the ceiling around 58 fps at 13 ms/frame of draw;
  GPU projection is what would raise that.

❯ smooth now, make blend the default and drop derive

● Done and deployed (0520e346). Interpolation is now the default, so the game
  renders at display rate (~58 fps measured) instead of the 18.9 fps tick rate.
  ?interp=0 restores tick-rate drawing.

  cam= is gone entirely — the camera is always interpolated between its own two
  tick states, over the same t as the objects, so the two can't disagree by
  construction. Re-derive was structurally unfixable: follow() is a stateful
  ease rather than a function of the interpolation fraction, so it applied
  exactly one ease step from the restored tick state each frame and lurched once
  per tick no matter how smooth the cars were.

  The launcher's Camera dropdown is removed, and "Frame interpolation" is now a
  ticked-by-default checkbox that emits interp=0 when you untick it. That needed
  a real distinction in the URL builder, which previously had one notion of "=0
  checkbox": the diagnostic stubs are off by default so ticking them emits =0,
  whereas interpolation is on by default so unticking it does. They're separate
  sets now, including when restoring state from a URL.

  104 tests pass — including the differential ones, which is what confirms the
  fractional sin/cos left the simulation bit-identical.

  Two notes on what this changes downstream:

  - Frame rate is now bounded by draw cost, not by the tick rate: ~13 ms/frame
  at 79% of a core puts the ceiling near 60 fps. GPU projection is the thing
  that would lift it further, and it's now the only remaining item in the
  performance list.
  - WORK.md's guidance still holds but for a new reason: fps is a meaningful
  comparison metric again because interpolation is on. With ?interp=0 it remains
  pinned at 18.9 and tells you nothing.

  changes, and the doc updates.

● Fixed and deployed (a9d76943). Both were the same regression from making
  interpolation the default, and my earlier HUD fix only covered half of it.

  The HUD is two kinds of drawing, not one. Labels and images go to the 2D
  overlay; the damage/power bars and the checkpoint arrow are geometry, in the
  same batch as the scene. Both are emitted once per tick from simulate(). My
  keepOverlay fix saved the text but the interpolated redraw still reset the
  geometry batch and drew only the scene — so the bars lost their fill (leaving
  the frame from the label image, which reads as "stuck at zero") and the arrow
  vanished entirely.

  Rather than re-run stat() — which is part of the simulation and has side
  effects — the batcher now snapshots the vertices simulate() produced and
  re-appends them after the interpolated scene draw. That preserves their
  content and their position in submission order, so they stay on top, which
  matters given there's no depth buffer. They update at tick rate, which is the
  rate they were drawn at anyway.

  Verified: bars are identical between interp=0 and interp=1.

  That's a good lesson recorded in WORK.md — the failure presented as a game bug
  (meters reading zero) when it was frame composition, and I'd have caught it
  earlier by asking "what else does simulate() draw?" rather than just "where
  does the HUD text go?"

  Not committed yet — say the word.

● Both done, committed as 01dae42 and deployed (40d73a6a).

  Sound effects — web/audio.js decodes sounds.zip and plays one-shots.
  AudioClip's contract ("play fires a one-shot, stop cuts it") maps cleanly onto
  a fresh BufferSource per playback, so crash/skid/scrape/gscrape port from the
  Java unchanged, including the rotation counters that stop a sample repeating
  back-to-back and the bfXXX debounce counters. Two Web Audio impositions the
  applet didn't have: the context starts suspended (unlocked on first key press)
  and decoding is async (an undecoded clip is skipped rather than stalling the
  tick). Missing sounds.zip or no Web Audio costs sound and nothing else.

  One trap worth flagging since it'll recur with any other zip asset:
  decodeAudioData detaches the buffer it's given, and the zip reader hands out
  views into one shared ArrayBuffer — decoding one clip would have invalidated
  every other. Each is sliced first.

  decompilation/MUSIC_PORT_SPEC.md specs the ibxm port for agy -p, in agy_tmp's
  shape. The argument for delegating it: 2,494 lines across 11 files, no
  renderer contact (so the painter's-algorithm rule can't be violated), no
  shared game state, and — unusually for this project — an exact oracle: render
  N seconds of PCM through the real classes by reflection and diff against the
  JS as integers. That's what makes it safe to hand over, since PORT_SPEC.md's
  objection to delegating numeric work is precisely that errors are plausible
  rather than detectable.

  The spec bounds it as calibrate-before-batching demands (Data.java first for
  the sign-extension traps, then Sample/Envelope for the int/float mixing, only
  then the rest), keeps the AudioWorklet wiring for you rather than the agent,
  and restates the rules that have actually been broken before — §2c, and
  WORK.md's record of tests being edited green twice and loadcar silently
  stubbed.

  I did not verify the sound by ear — headless has no audio device, so I
  confirmed only that the clips decode without error and the game still runs.
  Worth a listen.

● TASKS.md now has an "Audio — next session" section with the work in dependency
  order, committed as 5c9dc7d.

  The ordering matters, so it's explicit about why:

  1. playsounds() first — everything else depends on it. xtGraphics.java:9081,
  ~200 lines, called once per tick from GameSparker.java:1705. It decrements all
  five bfXXX debounce counters, and nothing in the port does. That's why the
  game sounds nearly silent: you get exactly one crash, one skid and one scrape
  per session. Small fix, large effect.
  2. Engine sound, which lives in the same method — engs[][].checkopen()
  switching the numbered samples (00–44 = gear × rev, plus air0–air5) as
  continuous loops. Needs a looping clip type in web/audio.js; the one-shot
  backend can't express AudioClip.loop().
  3. The untriggered one-shots — checkpoint, wasted, firewasted, carfixed,
  powerup, and the one/two/three/go countdown. They decode fine already; their
  call sites are inside playsounds() and the race-state code.
  4. Music, with decompilation/MUSIC_PORT_SPEC.md as the delegation spec.

  The playsounds() trap is also in WORK.md, since "ported the sound methods, got
  one sound" is exactly the kind of thing that reads as a browser audio problem
  when it's a missing tick pump.

● Found it. fixit() advances this.fcnt and clears this.fix when it exceeds 7 —

● Fixed, committed (4383449), deployed (44563e83).

  ContO.fixit() steps fcnt and clears fix once it passes 7 — and it runs from
  ContO.d(), i.e. from draw(), which now runs at display rate instead of tick
  rate. So the sparkle animation advanced several times per tick and could step
  straight past the single frame where fcnt > 7 clears fix, leaving the car
  sparkling until the next repair reset it. That's exactly the "occasionally
  gets stuck, repairing again fixes it" behaviour.

  This is the per-object counterpart of the MED_STATE snapshot that already
  existed for Medium's per-frame visual state. WORK.md recorded
  "GameSparker.draw() is NOT pure" for Medium.d(); it's equally true per object,
  and I'd only handled the Medium half. fcnt and fix now travel with the
  transform in the capture/restore, and WORK.md says so.

  Worth flagging for the next session: there are probably more of these.
  electrify() walks elc/edl/edr, and the dust/spark system has stg[] — all
  driven from d(), all unrestored. They'd show up as effects running fast or
  sticking, which reads as a game bug rather than a frame-composition one. If
  you see any other animation misbehaving under interpolation, that's the first
  place to look, and the fix is a one-word addition to OBJ_STATE.

● Same cause, opposite symptom: stg[] advances one step per ContO.d() call, so
  at display rate the puffs run through their 10 stages ~3× too fast. Those are
  arrays, so the snapshot needs to copy them:

  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

❯ before spending the last of our usage, can we decide why these keep popping   
  up?  one sentence                                                             

● Because the interpolation design re-runs draw(), which is not a renderer but a
  mutator — every effect in the game advances its own counter from inside
  ContO.d()/Medium.d() — so instead of enumerating that state once (or making
  the interpolated pass non-mutating, e.g. by capturing the tick's vertex buffer
  and re-projecting it rather than re-executing the draw), we've been patching
  it field by field as each effect visibly breaks.

❯ is this what we should live with, or is there a more idiomatic approach to    
  get them all?  second question: some sound effects only play once then are    
  silent for rest of race (e.g. crash into ground sound)..  is this expected?   
                                                                                
  one sentence each                                                             

● Approach: No — the idiomatic fix is to stop enumerating state and instead mark
  the pass: set an interpolating flag for the redraw and guard each counter
  advance at its source (if (!m.interpolating) ++this.fcnt), so a missed effect
  is a one-line fix visible right where the mutation happens rather than a field
  you forgot to add to a snapshot list in another file — and the radical
  version is to not re-execute draw() at all, keeping the tick's vertex buffer
  and re-projecting it, which eliminates the whole class.

  Sound: Not expected, and it's the bug already at the top of the audio list —
  playsounds() (xtGraphics.java:9081) is what decrements bfcrash/bfskid/bfscrape
  each tick, nothing in the port does, so the first crash sets bfcrash = 2 and
  every later one is suppressed for the rest of the race.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Both recorded and committed as 006fd48.

  Rendering — filed as a design item rather than another audit: thread an
  interpolating flag through the redraw and guard the counter advances at their
  source, so a missed effect is a one-line fix where the mutation lives instead
  of a field forgotten in a snapshot list in another file. The stronger option
  (keep the tick's vertex buffer and re-project rather than re-executing draw())
  is noted as removing the class outright. electrify() is named as the one
  still unfixed.

  Audio — listed as its own line just above playsounds(), because "a sound plays
  once then never again" presents as an audio bug, not as a missing method, and
  whoever hits it next shouldn't have to rediscover the connection.

  Working tree is clean apart from data/user.data (the game rewrites it at
  runtime) and your two chats/ files.
